Like, at the shop, we have this tool crib system. You need a specific die for the hydraulic press break? You gotta go to the crib, hand over your brass tag with your employee number on it, and the crib attendant gives you the die. They hang your tag on the peg where the tool goes. That way, everyone knows exactly who has what. It's foolproof. Unless, of course, the attendant gets lazy and just hands out tools without taking tags. We had this kid, Jason, working the crib a few years back. Nice kid, but his head was in the clouds. He let half the shop walk away with micrometers and calipers without tagging them. End of the month inventory comes around, and we're missing like three grand in precision measuring tools. Management was furious. They had to threaten to dock everyone's pay before guys started bringing stuff back from their toolboxes. \n\nThat's the thing about organization, it only works if you enforce it. You have to maintain the system. So yeah, I lock my stuff up now. I shouldn't have to in my own home, but I do. I'm keeping control the best I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife is involved in or aware of her relative's actions against you?\n\nPatient: My wife? Involved? No, I don't... I don't think she's in on it. I mean, she tells me I'm crazy, that I'm making it all up, but I don't think she's helping him mess with my stuff. She just doesn't see it. Or doesn't want to see it, maybe. \n\nIt's kind of like when you're trying to explain to an engineer why their blueprint won't work in the real world. They draw these things up on CAD, right? Perfect 90-degree angles, tolerances tighter than a gnat's behind. But they don't factor in springback when you're bending sheet metal. You take a piece of 10-gauge mild steel, put it in the press brake, and bend it to 90 degrees. When the die releases, the metal springs back a couple of degrees. It always does. You have to overbend it to like 92 or 93 degrees to get a true 90. \n\nI had this one engineer fresh out of college—wore these shiny loafers on the shop floor, which is a huge safety hazard, by the way. If you drop a piece of heavy plate on your foot without steel toes, you're losing toes. Simple as that. Anyway, he kept rejecting my parts because I was bending them past his specified angle on the print. I tried to explain springback to him, but he just tapped his clipboard and said, \"Follow the print.\" So I did. I followed it exactly. And of course, every single part failed quality control because they were all sitting at 88 degrees instead of 90. The assembly guys couldn't get the bolts to line up. It was a whole disaster, shut down the line for a day. \n\nSometimes people just look at the paper and don't see the reality right in front of them. That's my wife with this whole thing. She's looking at the blueprint of her perfect family, and she doesn't see the metal springing back. She doesn't see what he's actually doing. But no, I don't think she's the one hiding my tools.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your wife's relative messing with your tools is not based on real events?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. Like I said earlier, I'm really only sitting in this chair because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining things. But it's based on real events. Real life. My tools don't just get up and walk away on their own, and my fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It’s been months of this, and it’s plain as day to me that he’s just trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nAnd you know, speaking of the fishing... I was supposed to take my skiff out to the reservoir last weekend. I actually fabricated the trailer for that boat myself. Welded the whole thing out of three-inch channel iron, completely custom. But the thing about boat trailers is you have to be religious about the wheel bearings. You back a hot hub into cold water at the boat ramp, and it creates a vacuum—sucks the lake water right past the seal if you aren't careful. You have to use marine-grade grease. I use the red tacky stuff, Lucas Oil usually. \n\nMy buddy Dave, he tried using regular all-purpose lithium grease on his pontoon trailer once just to save a couple of bucks. I told him, I said, 'Dave, that's gonna wash right out the second it hits the water.' And sure enough, mid-July, he's stuck on the shoulder of Route 9, calling me to bring my oxy-acetylene torch because the bearing got so hot it friction-welded the inner race right to the spindle. We spent three hours out there in the blazing sun just carefully cutting that thing off without nicking the axle. You just can't cut corners on your maintenance like that, or the road will make you pay for it. You gotta do things right the first time.\n\nSo... anyway. What were you asking? Oh, if it's not real. No. No, I'm not making any of this up. He's doing it, and I know he's doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your wife's relative is not actually doing these things to you, and that you might be misinterpreting what's happening?\n\nPatient: I don't... Doc, we just went over this. I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it, but I know he came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't just get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's not a misinterpretation. After all these months, I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you're setting up a TIG welder for aluminum. You can't just guess at the settings and hope for the best. You need AC power, not DC, because the positive half of the cycle cleans the aluminum oxide off the surface, and the negative half actually penetrates the metal. If you try to run DC straight polarity like you would on steel, you'll just melt the tungsten electrode right into the puddle and make a huge mess. \n\nI was trying to teach my nephew how to TIG weld a few years ago. Good kid, but zero patience. He thought he could just grab the torch and start laying dimes without prepping the metal. With aluminum, you have to hit it with a stainless steel wire brush first—and it has to be a brush you only use on aluminum, otherwise you cross-contaminate it with carbon steel and the weld gets all porous and weak. I turned my back for two minutes to grab some filler rod, and he's over there trying to weld through a layer of grease and oxidation. The puddle was sputtering, spitting black soot everywhere. I had to make him grind the whole thing out and start over. You can't just ignore the reality of what's happening right in front of you and expect a good result. \n\nSo, no. I'm not misinterpreting anything. He's doing it. I know he is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your wife's relative is something that you cannot change, no matter what evidence or reasoning is presented to you?\n\nPatient: I don't... look, Doc, evidence? Reasoning? You can't reason away what's right in front of your face. I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it, but I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this. I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. There's no changing my mind because it's the truth. \n\nIt's like trying to tell me a piece of 304 stainless isn't going to warp if you put too much heat into it. People think stainless is tough, right? They think they can just lay into it with a MIG gun and run a continuous bead down a long joint. But stainless has terrible thermal conductivity. The heat just sits there in the weld zone, and before you know it, the metal is pulling and twisting like a pretzel. I've seen guys ruin thousands of dollars of material because they didn't want to take the time to stagger their welds or use chill bars. \n\nI built this custom exhaust header for a guy's hot rod once—beautiful piece of work, all pie cuts, TIG welded. You have to back-purge the inside of the pipe with argon gas when you weld stainless tubing like that. If you don't, the oxygen gets to the backside of the weld and creates this nasty, crusty oxidation called \"sugaring.\" It looks exactly like burnt sugar, and it completely ruins the flow inside the pipe. Not to mention it makes the weld weak. This guy, he brought it back to me a year later, said it cracked. I looked at it, and I knew right away he had taken it to some muffler shop to have a bung welded on for an O2 sensor, and those guys didn't back-purge it. They just blasted it with a MIG welder. Sugared the whole inside of the tube, and it cracked right at the heat-affected zone. I told him, I said, \"You can't argue with metallurgy.\" The metal does what the metal does. \n\nAnd that's... that's what I'm saying here. You can't argue with what's actually happening. So no, I'm not going to change my mind, because I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Are you open to the possibility that there could be another explanation for the things happening to your tools and plans, other than your wife's relative being involved?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. How many different ways can you ask me the same question? No, there is no other explanation. I'm only sitting in this office because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into my house to get at me. Tools do not just sprout legs and walk off. My fishing plans don't get sabotaged every single weekend by some magical coincidence. It's been going on for months, and it is plain as day to me that he's trying to push me out and take over my place. \n\nYou ask about other explanations... it’s like... well, take my Starrett micrometer. You know what a micrometer is, right? Precision measuring tool. Reads down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. I keep mine in a felt-lined wooden box, top drawer of my Kennedy toolbox. Now, one morning a few years back, I pull it out to check the diameter on a piece of turned cold-rolled steel, and it's reading three-thousandths under. Now, steel doesn't just shrink overnight. There's no mysterious \"other explanation\" for that. Somebody dropped it. \n\nTurned out, the new guy on the floor—guy named Gary—had borrowed it on second shift without asking, dropped it smack on the concrete, and put it back hoping I wouldn't notice. You drop a precision instrument like that, you spring the frame. Even just a hair, and the whole thing is garbage until you fix it. I had to send it all the way back to the factory in Massachusetts to get the anvil lapped and recalibrated. Cost me a fortune, not to mention the downtime. You can't just pretend a dropped mic is fine, and you can't just make up some fantasy explanation when the anvil is visibly out of square. \n\nGary didn't last long after that. You just... you have to respect another man's tools. If a guy doesn't respect your tools, he doesn't respect you. It's just a rule of the shop. You don't touch what isn't yours. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know why we keep going over this. There's no other explanation. He's doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, what could anyone possibly say? We are just beating a dead horse at this point. Like I said, I am only here because everybody keeps acting like I am imagining the whole thing. But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It has been months of this, and I can see plain as day he has been trying to take over my place and push me out. No one can tell me different because the proof is right there.

It is exactly like... well, take tramming a Bridgeport milling machine. You ever run a knee mill? You can't just trust someone who walks up and says the head is square. You have to tram it yourself. You put a dial indicator in the spindle and you sweep the table, front to back, left to right. If that needle moves even a thousandth of an inch, you are not perpendicular. I had a foreman once, back at the old shop, trying to tell me the mill was good to go. I ran a fly cutter across a block of aluminum, and it left a step you could catch your fingernail on. A blind man could feel it. 

But this foreman, he just kept insisting it was in spec. He didn't want to take the time to loosen the four bolts and tap the head into alignment. People get lazy, you know? They don't want to deal with the reality of the setup. They want to rush the job, ship the parts, and go home. But if you are machining a mating surface for a hydraulic manifold, and you leave a step like that, the seal is going to blow out under pressure. You will have hydraulic fluid spraying everywhere at three thousand PSI, all because some guy didn't want to spend ten minutes with an indicator. 

So... what was the question? Right, no. Nobody can tell me anything to make me question it. I know what is happening in my own house just like I know my own machines.

That way, everyone knows exactly who has what. It's foolproof. Unless, of course, the attendant gets lazy and just hands out tools without taking tags. You take a piece of 10-gauge mild steel, put it in the press brake, and bend it to 90 degrees. When the die releases, the metal springs back a couple of degrees. It always does. We spent three hours out there in the blazing sun just carefully cutting that thing off without nicking the axle. You can't just guess at the settings and hope for the best. You need AC power, not DC, because the positive half of the cycle cleans the aluminum oxide off the surface, and the negative half actually penetrates the metal. If you try to run DC straight polarity like you would on steel, you'll just melt the tungsten electrode right into the puddle and make a huge mess. \n\nI was trying to teach my nephew how to TIG weld a few years ago. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this. I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. There's no changing my mind because it's the truth. \n\nIt's like trying to tell me a piece of 304 stainless isn't going to warp if you put too much heat into it. People think stainless is tough, right? They think they can just lay into it with a MIG gun and run a continuous bead down a long joint. But stainless has terrible thermal conductivity. The heat just sits there in the weld zone, and before you know it, the metal is pulling and twisting like a pretzel. I've seen guys ruin thousands of dollars of material because they didn't want to take the time to stagger their welds or use chill bars. \n\nI built this custom exhaust header for a guy's hot rod once—beautiful piece of work, all pie cuts, TIG welded. You have to back-purge the inside of the pipe with argon gas when you weld stainless tubing like that. If you don't, the oxygen gets to the backside of the weld and creates this nasty, crusty oxidation called \"sugaring.\" It looks exactly like burnt sugar, and it completely ruins the flow inside the pipe. Not to mention it makes the weld weak. This guy, he brought it back to me a year later, said it cracked. I looked at it, and I knew right away he had taken it to some muffler shop to have a bung welded on for an O2 sensor, and those guys didn't back-purge it. They just blasted it with a MIG welder. Sugared the whole inside of the tube, and it cracked right at the heat-affected zone. I told him, I said, \"You can't argue with metallurgy.\" The metal does what the metal does. \n\nAnd that's... that's what I'm saying here. You can't argue with what's actually happening. So no, I'm not going to change my mind, because I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Are you open to the possibility that there could be another explanation for the things happening to your tools and plans, other than your wife's relative being involved?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into my house to get at me. Tools do not just sprout legs and walk off. It's been going on for months, and it is plain as day to me that he's trying to push me out and take over my place. \n\nYou ask about other explanations... it’s like... well, take my Starrett micrometer. There's no mysterious \"other explanation\" for that. Somebody dropped it. \n\nTurned out, the new guy on the floor—guy named Gary—had borrowed it on second shift without asking, dropped it smack on the concrete, and put it back hoping I wouldn't notice. You drop a precision instrument like that, you spring the frame. Even just a hair, and the whole thing is garbage until you fix it. I had to send it all the way back to the factory in Massachusetts to get the anvil lapped and recalibrated. Cost me a fortune, not to mention the downtime. You can't just pretend a dropped mic is fine, and you can't just make up some fantasy explanation when the anvil is visibly out of square. \n\nGary didn't last long after that. You just... you have to respect another man's tools. If a guy doesn't respect your tools, he doesn't respect you. It's just a rule of the shop. You don't touch what isn't yours. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know why we keep going over this. There's no other explanation. He's doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anything someone could tell you that would make you question your belief about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Doc, what could anyone possibly say? But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It has been months of this, and I can see plain as day he has been trying to take over my place and push me out. No one can tell me different because the proof is right there.\n\nIt is exactly like... well, take tramming a Bridgeport milling machine. You ever run a knee mill? You can't just trust someone who walks up and says the head is square. You have to tram it yourself. You put a dial indicator in the spindle and you sweep the table, front to back, left to right. If that needle moves even a thousandth of an inch, you are not perpendicular. I had a foreman once, back at the old shop, trying to tell me the mill was good to go. I ran a fly cutter across a block of aluminum, and it left a step you could catch your fingernail on. A blind man could feel it. \n\nBut this foreman, he just kept insisting it was in spec. He didn't want to take the time to loosen the four bolts and tap the head into alignment. People get lazy, you know? They don't want to deal with the reality of the setup. They want to rush the job, ship the parts, and go home. But if you are machining a mating surface for a hydraulic manifold, and you leave a step like that, the seal is going to blow out under pressure. You will have hydraulic fluid spraying everywhere at three thousand PSI, all because some guy didn't want to spend ten minutes with an indicator. \n\nSo... what was the question? Right, no. I don't... Doc, how many times do we have to go around this track? No. Nothing's going to change my mind because I'm not making it up. I'm only here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. Tools don't just move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this, and I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. 

It’s like... look, it’s like trying to tell me a piece of sheet metal doesn't have a grain direction. You ever work a press brake? You take a piece of aluminum, say 5052-H32, and you try to put a tight radius bend parallel to the grain, it’s going to crack right down the bend line. Every time. It doesn't matter what you believe, or what somebody tells you, or what you *want* it to do. The material has properties. You have to bend perpendicular to the grain if you want it to hold up. 

I had this apprentice once, kid named Tommy. Good kid, but he just didn't want to listen. He had this whole stack of brackets to bend up for a custom HVAC ducting job. I told him, I said, "Tommy, look at the shear marks. Watch your grain." But he was in a hurry. Wanted to get out early for a concert or something. He bent the whole run—fifty brackets—parallel to the grain. Every single one of them had micro-fractures along the outside radius. You could see it if you just looked close. I had to scrap the whole batch. Cost the shop a couple hundred bucks in material and a whole afternoon of labor. 

You can't just wish away the facts. The metal tells you what's real. And that's what I'm saying about my house. The facts are the facts. He's doing it, and nothing is going to change my mind because I'm looking right at the cracked aluminum, so to speak.

I had to make him grind the whole thing out and start over. You can't just ignore the reality of what's happening right in front of you and expect a good result. \n\nSo, no. I'm not misinterpreting anything. He's doing it. I know he is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your wife's relative is something that you cannot change, no matter what evidence or reasoning is presented to you?\n\nPatient: I don't... look, Doc, evidence? Reasoning? You can't reason away what's right in front of your face. I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it, but I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this. I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. There's no changing my mind because it's the truth. \n\nIt's like trying to tell me a piece of 304 stainless isn't going to warp if you put too much heat into it. People think stainless is tough, right? They think they can just lay into it with a MIG gun and run a continuous bead down a long joint. But stainless has terrible thermal conductivity. The heat just sits there in the weld zone, and before you know it, the metal is pulling and twisting like a pretzel. I've seen guys ruin thousands of dollars of material because they didn't want to take the time to stagger their welds or use chill bars. \n\nI built this custom exhaust header for a guy's hot rod once—beautiful piece of work, all pie cuts, TIG welded. You have to back-purge the inside of the pipe with argon gas when you weld stainless tubing like that. If you don't, the oxygen gets to the backside of the weld and creates this nasty, crusty oxidation called \"sugaring.\" It looks exactly like burnt sugar, and it completely ruins the flow inside the pipe. Not to mention it makes the weld weak. This guy, he brought it back to me a year later, said it cracked. I looked at it, and I knew right away he had taken it to some muffler shop to have a bung welded on for an O2 sensor, and those guys didn't back-purge it. They just blasted it with a MIG welder. Sugared the whole inside of the tube, and it cracked right at the heat-affected zone. I told him, I said, \"You can't argue with metallurgy.\" The metal does what the metal does. \n\nAnd that's... that's what I'm saying here. You can't argue with what's actually happening. So no, I'm not going to change my mind, because I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Are you open to the possibility that there could be another explanation for the things happening to your tools and plans, other than your wife's relative being involved?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. How many different ways can you ask me the same question? No, there is no other explanation. I'm only sitting in this office because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into my house to get at me. Tools do not just sprout legs and walk off. My fishing plans don't get sabotaged every single weekend by some magical coincidence. It's been going on for months, and it is plain as day to me that he's trying to push me out and take over my place. \n\nYou ask about other explanations... it’s like... well, take my Starrett micrometer. You know what a micrometer is, right? Precision measuring tool. Reads down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. I keep mine in a felt-lined wooden box, top drawer of my Kennedy toolbox. Now, one morning a few years back, I pull it out to check the diameter on a piece of turned cold-rolled steel, and it's reading three-thousandths under. Now, steel doesn't just shrink overnight. There's no mysterious \"other explanation\" for that. Somebody dropped it. \n\nTurned out, the new guy on the floor—guy named Gary—had borrowed it on second shift without asking, dropped it smack on the concrete, and put it back hoping I wouldn't notice. You drop a precision instrument like that, you spring the frame. Even just a hair, and the whole thing is garbage until you fix it. I had to send it all the way back to the factory in Massachusetts to get the anvil lapped and recalibrated. Cost me a fortune, not to mention the downtime. You can't just pretend a dropped mic is fine, and you can't just make up some fantasy explanation when the anvil is visibly out of square. \n\nGary didn't last long after that. You just... you have to respect another man's tools. If a guy doesn't respect your tools, he doesn't respect you. It's just a rule of the shop. You don't touch what isn't yours. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know why we keep going over this. There's no other explanation. He's doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anything someone could tell you that would make you question your belief about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Doc, what could anyone possibly say? We are just beating a dead horse at this point. Like I said, I am only here because everybody keeps acting like I am imagining the whole thing. But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It has been months of this, and I can see plain as day he has been trying to take over my place and push me out. No one can tell me different because the proof is right there.\n\nIt is exactly like... well, take tramming a Bridgeport milling machine. You ever run a knee mill? You can't just trust someone who walks up and says the head is square. You have to tram it yourself. You put a dial indicator in the spindle and you sweep the table, front to back, left to right. If that needle moves even a thousandth of an inch, you are not perpendicular. I had a foreman once, back at the old shop, trying to tell me the mill was good to go. I ran a fly cutter across a block of aluminum, and it left a step you could catch your fingernail on. A blind man could feel it. \n\nBut this foreman, he just kept insisting it was in spec. He didn't want to take the time to loosen the four bolts and tap the head into alignment. People get lazy, you know? They don't want to deal with the reality of the setup. They want to rush the job, ship the parts, and go home. But if you are machining a mating surface for a hydraulic manifold, and you leave a step like that, the seal is going to blow out under pressure. You will have hydraulic fluid spraying everywhere at three thousand PSI, all because some guy didn't want to spend ten minutes with an indicator. \n\nSo... what was the question? Right, no. Nobody can tell me anything to make me question it. I know what is happening in my own house just like I know my own machines.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen that would make you change your mind about your wife's relative being responsible for the things happening to your tools and plans?\n\nPatient: I don't... Doc, how many times do we have to go around this track? No. Nothing's going to change my mind because I'm not making it up. I'm only here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. Tools don't just move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this, and I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nIt’s like... look, it’s like trying to tell me a piece of sheet metal doesn't have a grain direction. You ever work a press brake? You take a piece of aluminum, say 5052-H32, and you try to put a tight radius bend parallel to the grain, it’s going to crack right down the bend line. Every time. It doesn't matter what you believe, or what somebody tells you, or what you *want* it to do. The material has properties. You have to bend perpendicular to the grain if you want it to hold up. \n\nI had this apprentice once, kid named Tommy. Good kid, but he just didn't want to listen. He had this whole stack of brackets to bend up for a custom HVAC ducting job. I told him, I said, \"Tommy, look at the shear marks. Watch your grain.\" But he was in a hurry. Wanted to get out early for a concert or something. He bent the whole run—fifty brackets—parallel to the grain. Every single one of them had micro-fractures along the outside radius. You could see it if you just looked close. I had to scrap the whole batch. Cost the shop a couple hundred bucks in material and a whole afternoon of labor. \n\nYou can't just wish away the facts. The metal tells you what's real. And that's what I'm saying about my house. The facts are the facts. He's doing it, and nothing is going to change my mind because I'm looking right at the cracked aluminum, so to speak.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, are you deaf? Or is this some kind of psychological test where you just ask the exact same thing until I pass out? I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. I've told you, I am only here because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it! But I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. How much clearer can I be? My tools don’t move themselves. My fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every single weekend. After all these months, I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out. You asking me about "evidence" changing my mind...

It's like... look, it’s like trying to cut a thread on an engine lathe and ignoring the thread dial. You ever run a South Bend lathe? Say you're single-pointing a thread. If you're cutting an even number of threads per inch, like a 16 pitch, you can engage the half-nut on any line on the dial. But if you're cutting an odd thread, say 13 TPI, you can only engage on the numbered lines. You can't just pretend the rules don't apply. 

I worked with this guy, Frank, back in the nineties. He was turning a custom lead screw out of a beautiful piece of O1 tool steel. Expensive stock. I told him, "Frank, watch your dial, it's an odd pitch." But he thought he could just eyeball the engagement. He locks the half-nut on an unnumbered mark, and bam, he cross-threads the very first pass. Tears the metal right up. You can't just present "evidence" to the lathe that it should cut right when you engaged the gears wrong. The machine doesn't care about your theories. Frank actually tried to take a triangle file to it, trying to dress the threads by hand to make it look like it was just a rough finish. But I put a pitch gauge on it and it was a total disaster. 

Frank ended up transferring to the shipping department after that. He just couldn't hack it in the tool room. You have to respect the mechanics of the thing. If you don't respect the mechanics... 

Anyway. What was I saying? Right, your question. No. The answer is no. I'm not going to consider another possibility because I know what's happening right in front of my face.

It doesn't matter what you believe, or what somebody tells you, or what you *want* it to do. The material has properties. You have to bend perpendicular to the grain if you want it to hold up. \n\nI had this apprentice once, kid named Tommy. Good kid, but he just didn't want to listen. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, we're going in circles again. I don't know how many different ways I can say no. There is nothing that's going to make me doubt it, because I'm not making it up! I'm only sitting in this chair right now because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining the whole thing. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this, and I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. 

It’s like... you ever use a torque wrench on a cylinder head? You can't just guess the torque. You have to follow the sequence, usually starting from the center and working your way out in a spiral or a crisscross pattern. If you just start cranking down bolts on one end, you're going to warp the head. I saw a guy do that once on a Chevy 350 block. He just zipped them down with an impact gun. An impact gun! On a cylinder head! 

I told him, I said, "Buddy, you're going to blow a head gasket before that thing even gets out of the driveway." And sure enough, he gets it all buttoned up, fires it, and there's white smoke just pouring out the tailpipe. Coolant leaking right into the cylinders. You can't argue with the torque specs. The metal doesn't care if you think it's tight enough. It only cares about the actual foot-pounds. 

He ended up having to pull the whole top end apart again, take the heads to a machine shop to get them decked because they were warped so bad. Cost him a fortune. And he tried to blame the gasket! Said it was a defective gasket from the factory. I just shook my head. Some people just refuse to see what they did wrong. 

But yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right. Doubting it. No. I don't doubt it at all. The proof is right there in my house, just like the white smoke out of that tailpipe.

You leave a micrometer out at the shop, someone’s gonna walk off with it, or worse, it gets knocked out of calibration and ruins a whole batch of parts. I had a buddy, Tommy, who lost his favorite socket set that way back in '98, just left it on the bench and went to lunch. \n\nBut anyway, I know exactly where I put my things in my own garage. I actually built a custom smoker out of a 55-gallon food-grade drum just for that trip. Welded the hinges myself, used a step bit for the vents, and sealed it up real nice with high-temp silicone. I... I don't really know for sure what the end game is. I mean, disruption is one thing. It's like when the power company decides to cut the grid for maintenance without telling anybody. They did that to me last month, right in the middle of fixing up a trailer for my neighbor. \n\nHe’s got this big dual-axle utility rig, hauls a couple of heavy zero-turn mowers on it for his landscaping business. Really heavy duty stuff. You absolutely have to use a 7018 welding rod for that kind of structural weight, otherwise the weld is just gonna crack from all the road vibration. I learned that lesson the hard way back in my twenties, trying to patch up a buddy's boat trailer with a cheap little flux-core wire feed. We were going down the interstate and the whole tongue just snapped clean off. Sparks flying everywhere, cars swerving... it was a complete disaster. We had to wait three hours for a flatbed tow. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, what were we talking about? I just know I haven't figured out exactly what this guy wants yet, but he's definitely messing with my shop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this relative of your wife's is the only one who is against you, or are there others involved as well?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really seen anybody else poking around, so I guess it's just him. If you borrowed a tool, you put it back exactly where you found it, wiped down and oiled. No exceptions. One time, this new kid, maybe eighteen years old, borrowed Earl's favorite pair of tin snips and left them out in the rain overnight. No, absolutely not. I’m here because everybody keeps acting like I’m just imagining things, but I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. I mean, my tools don’t just get up and move themselves. I actually fabricated a whole new casting deck for it a few years back. You can't just slap cheap plywood on there; you gotta use solid aluminum sheet and buck rivets. None of those cheap pop rivets, or the whole thing will shear right off the second you hit a heavy wake. I spent three straight weeks getting the marine carpet glued down perfectly over the top of it. You have to use this heavy-duty solvent-based contact cement. But if you roll it out smooth and get the edges tucked under the gunwale, it lasts forever. Best casting platform I ever stood on... \n\nAnyway, what was I... right, the house. It's him.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that you couldn't explain, like voices or shadows, when you were alone in your house or garage?\n\nPatient: Voices or shadows? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my hearing is perfectly fine. I get it checked every year because of the shop noise. You work around angle grinders and plasma cutters all day, you absolutely have to wear proper hearing protection. I always use those custom-molded silicone earplugs. You mix these two putties together, stick 'em in your ears, and let 'em cure for ten minutes. They block out, like, 30 decibels. \n\nI remember this one time, we had a guy using a pneumatic chipping hammer on a massive steel I-beam without any plugs. Just raw-dogging the noise. The sound was bouncing off the concrete floors, echoing off the corrugated metal roof... it was deafening. He ended up with permanent tinnitus. Said it sounded like a tea kettle going off in his head 24/7. That's why I'm so careful. You only get one set of ears, you know? You gotta protect them. Same with your eyes. I never strike an arc without my auto-darkening hood down. I got this really nice Miller one, reacts in like one-twenty-thousandth of a second...\n\nBut, uh, anyway. No, I don't hear voices or see shadows or anything crazy like that. Just the very real fact that my tools are being messed with.\n\nDoctor: Why do you think your wife's relative is trying to mess with your tools and disrupt your plans?\n\nPatient: Why is he doing it? I don't know, honestly. I guess he just wants to push me out, like I said. Maybe he thinks if he ruins my stuff and drives me crazy, I'll just leave. But I don't really know his exact reasons, I just know what he's doing. \n\nIt’s like when you’re trying to weld a joint and the metal’s contaminated. You don’t always know exactly what kind of oil or grease got on there, but you know it’s ruining the weld. You get all this porosity, these little pinholes in the bead. It looks like Swiss cheese. I had a buddy, Frank, who tried to TIG weld a piece of aluminum tubing once without cleaning it first with acetone. Aluminum is tricky, man. It forms this oxide layer on the outside that melts at a way higher temperature than the actual aluminum underneath. So if you don't brush it with a dedicated stainless steel wire brush—and I mean a brush you only use for aluminum, never steel, or you'll cross-contaminate it—you just get this nasty, sputtering mess. Frank spent like two hours trying to lay a decent bead on this custom intake manifold he was building for his Chevy Nova. Beautiful car, by the way. Candy apple red, chopped top... \n\nBut yeah, the weld was just total garbage. He had to grind the whole thing out and start over. That's the thing about fabrication, you can't cut corners. You have to prep your materials. \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, his reasons. Yeah, I don't know why he's doing it, I just know he's the one messing with my stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility, no matter how small, that you might be mistaken about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? So if you don't brush it with a dedicated stainless steel wire brush—and I mean a brush you only use for aluminum, never steel, or you'll cross-contaminate it—you just get this nasty, sputtering mess. Frank spent like two hours trying to lay a decent bead on this custom intake manifold he was building for his Chevy Nova. Beautiful car, by the way. Candy apple red, chopped top... \n\nBut yeah, the weld was just total garbage. He had to grind the whole thing out and start over. That's the thing about fabrication, you can't cut corners. You have to prep your materials. \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, his reasons. Yeah, I don't know why he's doing it, I just know he's the one messing with my stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility, no matter how small, that you might be mistaken about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? No, absolutely not. I mean, I'm here because everyone keeps acting like I'm just imagining it, but I know what I know. I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't just move themselves, doc. And my fishing plans don't just get spoiled by accident every single weekend. After all these months, I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nIt's just like when you're setting up a jig for a production run. You know exactly where every toggle clamp goes, every stop block. If someone comes in and bumps your setup even a sixteenth of an inch, the whole run is ruined. I remember we were fabricating these aluminum frames for a batch of custom utility trailers. We had this massive welding table, two inches thick, Blanchard ground perfectly flat. We spent half the day getting the jig squared up, checking the diagonals with a tape measure, making sure everything was dead nuts on. Because if your frame isn't square, the axle won't track straight, and the trailer will dog-track down the highway, wearing out the tires in a thousand miles. \n\nAnyway, the night shift came in, and some guy decided to use our table to hammer out a bent piece of diamond plate. He must have smacked one of our stop blocks with his dead blow hammer, because the next morning, every single frame we tacked up was out of square. We had to cut all the tacks with a zip disc and start over. That's why you don't mess with another man's setup. \n\nSo, no, I'm not mistaken. I know exactly what's going on in my own house. He's doing it on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what happens in your house and with your belongings?\n\nPatient: In control? Well... I try to be. I mean, I've had to change how I do things. I don't leave my equipment out where I used to. I spend a lot of time locking everything up, checking the workshop constantly just to make sure things are where I left them. And I avoid being around him when he's at the house. It's made things real tense with my wife, honestly. But I'm still going to work, still paying the bills, you know? \n\nIt's just exhausting having to double-check everything. Like, at the shop, we have this tool crib system. You need a specific die for the hydraulic press break? You gotta go to the crib, hand over your brass tag with your employee number on it, and the crib attendant gives you the die. They hang your tag on the peg where the tool goes. That way, everyone knows exactly who has what. It's foolproof. Unless, of course, the attendant gets lazy and just hands out tools without taking tags. We had this kid, Jason, working the crib a few years back. Nice kid, but his head was in the clouds. He let half the shop walk away with micrometers and calipers without tagging them. End of the month inventory comes around, and we're missing like three grand in precision measuring tools. Management was furious. They had to threaten to dock everyone's pay before guys started bringing stuff back from their toolboxes. \n\nThat's the thing about organization, it only works if you enforce it. You have to maintain the system. So yeah, I lock my stuff up now. I shouldn't have to in my own home, but I do. I'm keeping control the best I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your wife is involved in or aware of her relative's actions against you?\n\nPatient: My wife? Involved? No, I don't... I don't think she's in on it. I mean, she tells me I'm crazy, that I'm making it all up, but I don't think she's helping him mess with my stuff. She just doesn't see it. Or doesn't want to see it, maybe. \n\nIt's kind of like when you're trying to explain to an engineer why their blueprint won't work in the real world. They draw these things up on CAD, right? Perfect 90-degree angles, tolerances tighter than a gnat's behind. But they don't factor in springback when you're bending sheet metal. You take a piece of 10-gauge mild steel, put it in the press brake, and bend it to 90 degrees. When the die releases, the metal springs back a couple of degrees. It always does. You have to overbend it to like 92 or 93 degrees to get a true 90. \n\nI had this one engineer fresh out of college—wore these shiny loafers on the shop floor, which is a huge safety hazard, by the way. If you drop a piece of heavy plate on your foot without steel toes, you're losing toes. Simple as that. Anyway, he kept rejecting my parts because I was bending them past his specified angle on the print. I tried to explain springback to him, but he just tapped his clipboard and said, \"Follow the print.\" So I did. I followed it exactly. And of course, every single part failed quality control because they were all sitting at 88 degrees instead of 90. The assembly guys couldn't get the bolts to line up. It was a whole disaster, shut down the line for a day. \n\nSometimes people just look at the paper and don't see the reality right in front of them. That's my wife with this whole thing. She's looking at the blueprint of her perfect family, and she doesn't see the metal springing back. She doesn't see what he's actually doing. But no, I don't think she's the one hiding my tools.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your wife's relative messing with your tools is not based on real events?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. Like I said earlier, I'm really only sitting in this chair because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining things. But it's based on real events. Real life. My tools don't just get up and walk away on their own, and my fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It’s been months of this, and it’s plain as day to me that he’s just trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nAnd you know, speaking of the fishing... I was supposed to take my skiff out to the reservoir last weekend. I actually fabricated the trailer for that boat myself. Welded the whole thing out of three-inch channel iron, completely custom. But the thing about boat trailers is you have to be religious about the wheel bearings. You back a hot hub into cold water at the boat ramp, and it creates a vacuum—sucks the lake water right past the seal if you aren't careful. You have to use marine-grade grease. I use the red tacky stuff, Lucas Oil usually. \n\nMy buddy Dave, he tried using regular all-purpose lithium grease on his pontoon trailer once just to save a couple of bucks. I told him, I said, 'Dave, that's gonna wash right out the second it hits the water.' And sure enough, mid-July, he's stuck on the shoulder of Route 9, calling me to bring my oxy-acetylene torch because the bearing got so hot it friction-welded the inner race right to the spindle. We spent three hours out there in the blazing sun just carefully cutting that thing off without nicking the axle. You just can't cut corners on your maintenance like that, or the road will make you pay for it. You gotta do things right the first time.\n\nSo... anyway. What were you asking? Oh, if it's not real. No. No, I'm not making any of this up. He's doing it, and I know he's doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your wife's relative is not actually doing these things to you, and that you might be misinterpreting what's happening?\n\nPatient: I don't... Doc, we just went over this. I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it, but I know he came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't just get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's not a misinterpretation. After all these months, I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nIt's like... it's like when you're setting up a TIG welder for aluminum. You can't just guess at the settings and hope for the best. You need AC power, not DC, because the positive half of the cycle cleans the aluminum oxide off the surface, and the negative half actually penetrates the metal. If you try to run DC straight polarity like you would on steel, you'll just melt the tungsten electrode right into the puddle and make a huge mess. \n\nI was trying to teach my nephew how to TIG weld a few years ago. Good kid, but zero patience. He thought he could just grab the torch and start laying dimes without prepping the metal. With aluminum, you have to hit it with a stainless steel wire brush first—and it has to be a brush you only use on aluminum, otherwise you cross-contaminate it with carbon steel and the weld gets all porous and weak. I turned my back for two minutes to grab some filler rod, and he's over there trying to weld through a layer of grease and oxidation. The puddle was sputtering, spitting black soot everywhere. I had to make him grind the whole thing out and start over. You can't just ignore the reality of what's happening right in front of you and expect a good result. \n\nSo, no. I'm not misinterpreting anything. He's doing it. I know he is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your wife's relative is something that you cannot change, no matter what evidence or reasoning is presented to you?\n\nPatient: I don't... look, Doc, evidence? Reasoning? You can't reason away what's right in front of your face. I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it, but I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this. I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. There's no changing my mind because it's the truth. \n\nIt's like trying to tell me a piece of 304 stainless isn't going to warp if you put too much heat into it. People think stainless is tough, right? They think they can just lay into it with a MIG gun and run a continuous bead down a long joint. But stainless has terrible thermal conductivity. The heat just sits there in the weld zone, and before you know it, the metal is pulling and twisting like a pretzel. I've seen guys ruin thousands of dollars of material because they didn't want to take the time to stagger their welds or use chill bars. \n\nI built this custom exhaust header for a guy's hot rod once—beautiful piece of work, all pie cuts, TIG welded. You have to back-purge the inside of the pipe with argon gas when you weld stainless tubing like that. If you don't, the oxygen gets to the backside of the weld and creates this nasty, crusty oxidation called \"sugaring.\" It looks exactly like burnt sugar, and it completely ruins the flow inside the pipe. Not to mention it makes the weld weak. This guy, he brought it back to me a year later, said it cracked. I looked at it, and I knew right away he had taken it to some muffler shop to have a bung welded on for an O2 sensor, and those guys didn't back-purge it. They just blasted it with a MIG welder. Sugared the whole inside of the tube, and it cracked right at the heat-affected zone. I told him, I said, \"You can't argue with metallurgy.\" The metal does what the metal does. \n\nAnd that's... that's what I'm saying here. You can't argue with what's actually happening. So no, I'm not going to change my mind, because I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Are you open to the possibility that there could be another explanation for the things happening to your tools and plans, other than your wife's relative being involved?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. How many different ways can you ask me the same question? No, there is no other explanation. I'm only sitting in this office because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into my house to get at me. Tools do not just sprout legs and walk off. My fishing plans don't get sabotaged every single weekend by some magical coincidence. It's been going on for months, and it is plain as day to me that he's trying to push me out and take over my place. \n\nYou ask about other explanations... it’s like... well, take my Starrett micrometer. You know what a micrometer is, right? Precision measuring tool. Reads down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. I keep mine in a felt-lined wooden box, top drawer of my Kennedy toolbox. Now, one morning a few years back, I pull it out to check the diameter on a piece of turned cold-rolled steel, and it's reading three-thousandths under. Now, steel doesn't just shrink overnight. There's no mysterious \"other explanation\" for that. Somebody dropped it. \n\nTurned out, the new guy on the floor—guy named Gary—had borrowed it on second shift without asking, dropped it smack on the concrete, and put it back hoping I wouldn't notice. You drop a precision instrument like that, you spring the frame. Even just a hair, and the whole thing is garbage until you fix it. I had to send it all the way back to the factory in Massachusetts to get the anvil lapped and recalibrated. Cost me a fortune, not to mention the downtime. You can't just pretend a dropped mic is fine, and you can't just make up some fantasy explanation when the anvil is visibly out of square. \n\nGary didn't last long after that. You just... you have to respect another man's tools. If a guy doesn't respect your tools, he doesn't respect you. It's just a rule of the shop. You don't touch what isn't yours. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know why we keep going over this. There's no other explanation. He's doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anything someone could tell you that would make you question your belief about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Doc, what could anyone possibly say? We are just beating a dead horse at this point. Like I said, I am only here because everybody keeps acting like I am imagining the whole thing. But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It has been months of this, and I can see plain as day he has been trying to take over my place and push me out. No one can tell me different because the proof is right there.\n\nIt is exactly like... well, take tramming a Bridgeport milling machine. You ever run a knee mill? You can't just trust someone who walks up and says the head is square. You have to tram it yourself. You put a dial indicator in the spindle and you sweep the table, front to back, left to right. If that needle moves even a thousandth of an inch, you are not perpendicular. I had a foreman once, back at the old shop, trying to tell me the mill was good to go. I ran a fly cutter across a block of aluminum, and it left a step you could catch your fingernail on. A blind man could feel it. \n\nBut this foreman, he just kept insisting it was in spec. He didn't want to take the time to loosen the four bolts and tap the head into alignment. People get lazy, you know? They don't want to deal with the reality of the setup. They want to rush the job, ship the parts, and go home. But if you are machining a mating surface for a hydraulic manifold, and you leave a step like that, the seal is going to blow out under pressure. You will have hydraulic fluid spraying everywhere at three thousand PSI, all because some guy didn't want to spend ten minutes with an indicator. \n\nSo... what was the question? Right, no. Nobody can tell me anything to make me question it. I know what is happening in my own house just like I know my own machines.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could happen that would make you change your mind about your wife's relative being responsible for the things happening to your tools and plans?\n\nPatient: I don't... Doc, how many times do we have to go around this track? No. Nothing's going to change my mind because I'm not making it up. I'm only here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. But I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. Tools don't just move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this, and I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nIt’s like... look, it’s like trying to tell me a piece of sheet metal doesn't have a grain direction. You ever work a press brake? You take a piece of aluminum, say 5052-H32, and you try to put a tight radius bend parallel to the grain, it’s going to crack right down the bend line. Every time. It doesn't matter what you believe, or what somebody tells you, or what you *want* it to do. The material has properties. You have to bend perpendicular to the grain if you want it to hold up. \n\nI had this apprentice once, kid named Tommy. Good kid, but he just didn't want to listen. He had this whole stack of brackets to bend up for a custom HVAC ducting job. I told him, I said, \"Tommy, look at the shear marks. Watch your grain.\" But he was in a hurry. Wanted to get out early for a concert or something. He bent the whole run—fifty brackets—parallel to the grain. Every single one of them had micro-fractures along the outside radius. You could see it if you just looked close. I had to scrap the whole batch. Cost the shop a couple hundred bucks in material and a whole afternoon of labor. \n\nYou can't just wish away the facts. The metal tells you what's real. And that's what I'm saying about my house. The facts are the facts. He's doing it, and nothing is going to change my mind because I'm looking right at the cracked aluminum, so to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever be able to consider the possibility that your wife's relative is not responsible for the things happening to your tools and plans, no matter what evidence is presented?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you deaf? Or is this some kind of psychological test where you just ask the exact same thing until I pass out? I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. I've told you, I am only here because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it! But I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. How much clearer can I be? My tools don’t move themselves. My fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every single weekend. After all these months, I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out. You asking me about \"evidence\" changing my mind...\n\nIt's like... look, it’s like trying to cut a thread on an engine lathe and ignoring the thread dial. You ever run a South Bend lathe? Say you're single-pointing a thread. If you're cutting an even number of threads per inch, like a 16 pitch, you can engage the half-nut on any line on the dial. But if you're cutting an odd thread, say 13 TPI, you can only engage on the numbered lines. You can't just pretend the rules don't apply. \n\nI worked with this guy, Frank, back in the nineties. He was turning a custom lead screw out of a beautiful piece of O1 tool steel. Expensive stock. I told him, \"Frank, watch your dial, it's an odd pitch.\" But he thought he could just eyeball the engagement. He locks the half-nut on an unnumbered mark, and bam, he cross-threads the very first pass. Tears the metal right up. You can't just present \"evidence\" to the lathe that it should cut right when you engaged the gears wrong. The machine doesn't care about your theories. Frank actually tried to take a triangle file to it, trying to dress the threads by hand to make it look like it was just a rough finish. But I put a pitch gauge on it and it was a total disaster. \n\nFrank ended up transferring to the shipping department after that. He just couldn't hack it in the tool room. You have to respect the mechanics of the thing. If you don't respect the mechanics... \n\nAnyway. What was I saying? Right, your question. No. The answer is no. I'm not going to consider another possibility because I know what's happening right in front of my face.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that could make you doubt your belief about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Doc, we're going in circles again. I don't know how many different ways I can say no. There is nothing that's going to make me doubt it, because I'm not making it up! I'm only sitting in this chair right now because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining the whole thing. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this, and I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. \n\nIt’s like... you ever use a torque wrench on a cylinder head? You can't just guess the torque. You have to follow the sequence, usually starting from the center and working your way out in a spiral or a crisscross pattern. If you just start cranking down bolts on one end, you're going to warp the head. I saw a guy do that once on a Chevy 350 block. He just zipped them down with an impact gun. An impact gun! On a cylinder head! \n\nI told him, I said, \"Buddy, you're going to blow a head gasket before that thing even gets out of the driveway.\" And sure enough, he gets it all buttoned up, fires it, and there's white smoke just pouring out the tailpipe. Coolant leaking right into the cylinders. You can't argue with the torque specs. The metal doesn't care if you think it's tight enough. It only cares about the actual foot-pounds. \n\nHe ended up having to pull the whole top end apart again, take the heads to a machine shop to get them decked because they were warped so bad. Cost him a fortune. And he tried to blame the gasket! Said it was a defective gasket from the factory. I just shook my head. Some people just refuse to see what they did wrong. \n\nBut yeah... what was I saying? Oh, right. Doubting it. No. I don't doubt it at all. The proof is right there in my house, just like the white smoke out of that tailpipe.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Breaks from reality? What? No, of course not. I do not hear things or see ghosts or whatever it is you are getting at. My eyes and ears work perfectly fine. You have to have sharp senses in my line of work. 

Take TIG welding, for instance. Tungsten Inert Gas. You are working with a foot pedal, filler rod in one hand, torch in the other. It is all about watching the weld pool. Especially on thin-gauge stainless. If your eyes are playing tricks on you, you are going to overheat the base metal and get sugaring on the back side. Sugaring is when the metal oxidizes because it did not have the argon purge gas covering it properly. It literally looks like burnt sugar. Ruins the whole joint, makes it weak. 

I worked with this guy, old Jimmy, back at the sheet metal plant. Jimmy was getting up there in years and just absolutely refused to get a cheater lens for his welding helmet. He would be running a bead on a sanitary pipe for a dairy facility, and he would swear up and down he was laying down a perfect stack of dimes. But he could not see the undercut. He was just guessing where the puddle was. The state inspector came through with a little flashlight and an inspection mirror and failed every single one of Jimmy's joints. Cost the company a whole week of rework. You cannot just imagine a good weld, you have to actually see the puddle and watch the way the metal flows. Jimmy ended up having to retire early because his eyes were just shot. 

But my eyes are fine. I pass my vision test for my driver license every time. So no, I do not see things that are not there. I see exactly what is right in front of me, which is how I know exactly what that guy is doing in my garage.

You need AC power, not DC, because the positive half of the cycle cleans the aluminum oxide off the surface, and the negative half actually penetrates the metal. If you try to run DC straight polarity like you would on steel, you'll just melt the tungsten electrode right into the puddle and make a huge mess. \n\nI was trying to teach my nephew how to TIG weld a few years ago. Good kid, but zero patience. He thought he could just grab the torch and start laying dimes without prepping the metal. With aluminum, you have to hit it with a stainless steel wire brush first—and it has to be a brush you only use on aluminum, otherwise you cross-contaminate it with carbon steel and the weld gets all porous and weak. I turned my back for two minutes to grab some filler rod, and he's over there trying to weld through a layer of grease and oxidation. The puddle was sputtering, spitting black soot everywhere. I had to make him grind the whole thing out and start over. You can't just ignore the reality of what's happening right in front of you and expect a good result. \n\nSo, no. I'm not misinterpreting anything. He's doing it. I know he is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your wife's relative is something that you cannot change, no matter what evidence or reasoning is presented to you?\n\nPatient: I don't... look, Doc, evidence? Reasoning? You can't reason away what's right in front of your face. I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it, but I know my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. My tools don't move themselves. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. It's been months of this. I can see plain as day he's been trying to take over my place and push me out. There's no changing my mind because it's the truth. \n\nIt's like trying to tell me a piece of 304 stainless isn't going to warp if you put too much heat into it. People think stainless is tough, right? They think they can just lay into it with a MIG gun and run a continuous bead down a long joint. But stainless has terrible thermal conductivity. The heat just sits there in the weld zone, and before you know it, the metal is pulling and twisting like a pretzel. I've seen guys ruin thousands of dollars of material because they didn't want to take the time to stagger their welds or use chill bars. \n\nI built this custom exhaust header for a guy's hot rod once—beautiful piece of work, all pie cuts, TIG welded. You have to back-purge the inside of the pipe with argon gas when you weld stainless tubing like that. If you don't, the oxygen gets to the backside of the weld and creates this nasty, crusty oxidation called \"sugaring.\" It looks exactly like burnt sugar, and it completely ruins the flow inside the pipe. Not to mention it makes the weld weak. This guy, he brought it back to me a year later, said it cracked. I looked at it, and I knew right away he had taken it to some muffler shop to have a bung welded on for an O2 sensor, and those guys didn't back-purge it. They just blasted it with a MIG welder. Sugared the whole inside of the tube, and it cracked right at the heat-affected zone. I told him, I said, \"You can't argue with metallurgy.\" The metal does what the metal does. \n\nAnd that's... that's what I'm saying here. You can't argue with what's actually happening. So no, I'm not going to change my mind, because I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Are you open to the possibility that there could be another explanation for the things happening to your tools and plans, other than your wife's relative being involved?\n\nPatient: Look, Doc, I feel like a broken record here. How many different ways can you ask me the same question? No, there is no other explanation. I'm only sitting in this office because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things. But I know for a fact my wife's relative came into my house to get at me. Tools do not just sprout legs and walk off. My fishing plans don't get sabotaged every single weekend by some magical coincidence. It's been going on for months, and it is plain as day to me that he's trying to push me out and take over my place. \n\nYou ask about other explanations... it’s like... well, take my Starrett micrometer. You know what a micrometer is, right? Precision measuring tool. Reads down to the ten-thousandth of an inch. I keep mine in a felt-lined wooden box, top drawer of my Kennedy toolbox. Now, one morning a few years back, I pull it out to check the diameter on a piece of turned cold-rolled steel, and it's reading three-thousandths under. Now, steel doesn't just shrink overnight. There's no mysterious \"other explanation\" for that. Somebody dropped it. \n\nTurned out, the new guy on the floor—guy named Gary—had borrowed it on second shift without asking, dropped it smack on the concrete, and put it back hoping I wouldn't notice. You drop a precision instrument like that, you spring the frame. Even just a hair, and the whole thing is garbage until you fix it. I had to send it all the way back to the factory in Massachusetts to get the anvil lapped and recalibrated. Cost me a fortune, not to mention the downtime. You can't just pretend a dropped mic is fine, and you can't just make up some fantasy explanation when the anvil is visibly out of square. \n\nGary didn't last long after that. You just... you have to respect another man's tools. If a guy doesn't respect your tools, he doesn't respect you. It's just a rule of the shop. You don't touch what isn't yours. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know why we keep going over this. There's no other explanation. He's doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen or anything someone could tell you that would make you question your belief about your wife's relative being responsible for these things?\n\nPatient: Doc, what could anyone possibly say?
